<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which amino-acid pair is identified as proton-accepting at the active site?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Histidine and glutamate<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Histidine and aspartate<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Asparagine and aspartate<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Lysine and arginine<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>To identify the proton-accepting pair at the active site, I need to analyze this protein sequence for its enzymatic identity and catalytic mechanism.<br><br>**Protein Identification:** The sequence shows characteristics of a dihydroorotase (DHOase, EC 3.5.2.3), a metalloenzyme involved in pyrimidine biosynthesis. Key features include the presence of conserved motifs like &quot;VQVDCTGEGVVFATARSGHYALD&quot; and &quot;HHS&quot; motifs typical of amidohydrolase family enzymes.<br><br>**Catalytic Analysis:** In dihydroorotase and related amidohydrolases, the catalytic mechanism involves:<br>- A metal-coordinating site (often zinc) coordinated by histidine and aspartate residues<br>- A separate His-Asp dyad that functions in proton transfer during catalysis<br><br>**Proton-accepting role:** In this enzyme family, histidine acts as a general base (proton acceptor), while aspartate serves to orient the histidine and modulate its pKa, enhancing its proton-accepting ability. This His-Asp dyad is a classic catalytic pair for proton transfer in many enzymes.<br><br>**Evaluating choices:**<br>- A. Histidine and glutamate – While possible in some enzymes, not the primary proton-accepting pair here<br>- B. Histidine and aspartate – This is the classic proton-accepting dyad found in dihydroorotase and many hydrolases<br>- C. Asparagine and aspartate – Asparagine is not typically a proton acceptor<br>- D. Lysine and arginine – These are basic residues but don&#x27;t typically form a catalytic proton-accepting pair<br><br>The His-Asp pair is well-documented as the proton-accepting dyad in this enzyme class.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this protein, which pair is specifically assigned proton-accepting roles at the active site? Choose based on the residue-specific catalytic role, not merely on which side chains could plausibly participate in acid–base chemistry.<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Histidine and glutamate<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Histidine and aspartate<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Asparagine and aspartate<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Lysine and arginine<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> active_site_0036:1<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>Let me analyze this protein sequence systematically.<br><br>**Sequence Analysis:**<br>- I notice the motif &quot;DFGWGSPAFMGPAIMYY&quot; near the C-terminus, which is characteristic of glycoside hydrolases.<br>- I also observe &quot;RHNGEPGFF&quot; containing a His (H) residue, and &quot;HHSIADGRSAAH&quot; with additional His residues.<br>- The overall architecture suggests a glycoside hydrolase family enzyme.<br><br>**Catalytic Mechanism Considerations:**<br>The question specifically asks about residues &quot;assigned proton-accepting roles&quot; — meaning residues functioning as general bases in the catalytic mechanism.<br><br>**Evaluating each option:**<br><br>- **A. Histidine and glutamate:** Glutamate typically functions as a general **acid** (proton donor) in catalytic dyads (e.g., GH families where Glu donates a proton to the leaving group), not as a proton acceptor.<br><br>- **B. Histidine and aspartate:** Both His and Asp can serve as proton acceptors (general bases) in enzyme catalysis. His is a classic general base (accepting protons from water nucleophiles or substrate hydroxyl groups), and Asp can also act as a proton acceptor or assist in proton shuttling. This pair represents a well-known charge-relay system where both residues participate in proton acceptance at different stages of catalysis.<br><br>- **C. Asparagine and aspartate:** Asparagine lacks the pKa properties needed for proton transfer catalysis — its side chain amide is not ionizable under physiological conditions and rarely participates in acid-base catalysis.<br><br>- **D. Lysine and arginine:** Both are positively charged at physiological pH and are essentially never proton acceptors (they are already protonated). They function as electrostatic stabilizers, not as bases.<br><br>**Conclusion:** His and Asp are the residues specifically assigned proton-accepting (base) roles in the catalytic center, as they can both accept protons during different phases of the reaction mechanism.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>
</tr></tbody>

RORA (retinoic acid receptor-related orphan receptor alpha) is a nuclear receptor. It does NOT typically have WQNP repeats in its canonical form. RORA has:

- A D domain (hinge)
- An E/F domain (LBD)
